From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

Country in Central Africa

Not to be confused with the geographical region [Central Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Africa "Central Africa").

"CAR" redirects here. For the vehicle, see [Car](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Car "Car"). For other uses, see [CAR (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CAR_(disambiguation) "CAR (disambiguation)").

The **Central African Republic**[&#8202;](#ref-back-8-0)[\[a\]](#refnote-8) ( **CAR**) is a [landlocked country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landlocked_country "Landlocked country") in [Central Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Africa "Central Africa"). It is bordered by [Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chad "Chad") to [the north](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Republic%E2%80%93Chad_border "Central African Republic–Chad border"), [Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudan "Sudan") to [the northeast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Republic%E2%80%93Sudan_border "Central African Republic–Sudan border"), [South Sudan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Sudan "South Sudan") to [the east](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Republic%E2%80%93South_Sudan_border "Central African Republic–South Sudan border"), the [Democratic Republic of the Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo "Democratic Republic of the Congo") to [the south](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Republic%E2%80%93Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo_border "Central African Republic–Democratic Republic of the Congo border"), the [Republic of the Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_the_Congo "Republic of the Congo") to [the southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Republic%E2%80%93Republic_of_the_Congo_border "Central African Republic–Republic of the Congo border"), and [Cameroon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroon "Cameroon") to [the west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cameroon%E2%80%93Central_African_Republic_border "Cameroon–Central African Republic border"). The CAR covers a land area of about 620,000 square kilometres (240,000sqmi). As of 2024, it has a population of 5,357,744, consisting of about 80 ethnic groups.[&#8202;](#ref-back-9-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-9) Having been a French colony under the name [Ubangi-Shari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubangi-Shari "Ubangi-Shari"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-10-0)[\[b\]](#refnote-10) [Sango](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sango_language "Sango language") is its national language and was also its [co-official language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Official_language "Official language") alongside [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language "French language").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Samarin_2000_301–334-1)[\[1\]](#refnote-Samarin_2000_301–334)[&#8202;](#ref-back-profile-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-profile) Its capital and largest city is [Bangui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangui "Bangui"), which sits on the country's southern border with the Democratic Republic of the Congo.

The CAR mainly consists of Sudano-Guinean [savanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savanna "Savanna"), but also includes a [Sahelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel "Sahel")- [Sudanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Sudanian_savanna "East Sudanian savanna") zone in the north and an [equatorial forest zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Congolian_forest%E2%80%93savanna_mosaic "Northern Congolian forest–savanna mosaic") in the south. Two-thirds of the country is within the basin of the [Ubangi River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubangi_River "Ubangi River") (a tributary of the [Congo River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congo_River "Congo River")), while the remaining third lies in the basin of the [Chari River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chari_River "Chari River"), which flows into [Lake Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Chad "Lake Chad").

The region has been inhabited since at least 8000 BCE. The borders were established by France, which began annexing portions to the [French Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Congo "French Congo") in the late 19th century and in 1903 established the separate colony of Ubangi-Shari, part of [French Equatorial Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Equatorial_Africa "French Equatorial Africa"). After gaining independence from France in 1960, the CAR was ruled by a series of autocratic leaders, including [Jean-Bédel Bokassa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-B%C3%A9del_Bokassa "Jean-Bédel Bokassa"), who changed the country's name to the [Central African Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Empire "Central African Empire") and ruled as a monarch from 1976 to 1979.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bokassa-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-Bokassa) The [Central African Bush War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Bush_War "Central African Bush War") began in 2004 and, despite a peace treaty in 2007 and another in 2011, [civil war resumed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Republic_Civil_War "Central African Republic Civil War") in 2012. The civil war perpetuated the country's poor [human rights record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_the_Central_African_Republic "Human rights in the Central African Republic"), characterized by widespread and increasing abuses by various participating armed groups.

Despite its vast [natural resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_resources "Natural resources"), such as [uranium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranium "Uranium") reserves, [crude oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crude_oil "Crude oil"), [gold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gold "Gold"), [diamonds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diamond "Diamond"), [cobalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cobalt "Cobalt"), [lumber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumber "Lumber"), and [hydropower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydropower "Hydropower"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-CIA-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-CIA) as well as significant quantities of [arable land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arable_land "Arable land"), the CAR is among the world's ten poorest countries, with the world's lowest [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_domestic_product "Gross domestic product") per capita at purchasing power parity (PPP) as of 2017.[&#8202;](#ref-back-IMF_2017-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-IMF_2017) As of 2023, according to the [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index "Human Development Index"), the country had the third-lowest level of human development, ranking 191st out of 193 countries. It had the second-lowest [inequality-adjusted Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inequality-adjusted_Human_Development_Index "Inequality-adjusted Human Development Index"), ranking 164th out of 165 countries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-HDI-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-HDI) It has been ranked as the unhealthiest country[&#8202;](#ref-back-16-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-16) and the worst country for young people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-17-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-17) The CAR is a member of the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations "United Nations"), the [African Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Union "African Union"), the [Economic Community of Central African States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_Community_of_Central_African_States "Economic Community of Central African States"), the [Organisation internationale de la Francophonie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_internationale_de_la_Francophonie "Organisation internationale de la Francophonie") and the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement "Non-Aligned Movement").

## Etymology

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=1 "Edit section: Etymology")\]

The name is derived from the country's geographical location in [Central Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Africa "Central Africa") and its [republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republicanism "Republicanism") form of government. From 1976 to 1979, the country was known as the [Central African Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Empire "Central African Empire").

During the colonial era, the country's name was [Ubangi-Shari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubangi-Shari "Ubangi-Shari") ( [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language "French language"): _Oubangui-Chari_), a name derived from two major rivers, the [Ubangi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubangi_River "Ubangi River") and the [Chari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chari_River "Chari River"). [Barthélemy Boganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barth%C3%A9lemy_Boganda "Barthélemy Boganda"), the first [prime minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heads_of_government_of_the_Central_African_Republic "List of heads of government of the Central African Republic"), favored the name "Central African Republic" over Ubangi-Shari, reportedly because he envisioned [a larger union of countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_of_Latin_Africa "United States of Latin Africa") in Central Africa.[&#8202;](#ref-back-18-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-18)

## History

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=2 "Edit section: History")\]

Main article: [History of the Central African Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Central_African_Republic "History of the Central African Republic")

### Early history

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=3 "Edit section: Early history")\]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/8/80/Bouar_Megaliths_stamp.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bouar_Megaliths_stamp.jpg) The [Bouar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bouar "Bouar") Megaliths, pictured here on a 1967 Central African stamp, date back to the very late Neolithic Era (c.3500–2700 BCE).

About 10,000 years ago, [desertification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertification "Desertification") forced [hunter-gatherer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer "Hunter-gatherer") societies south into the [Sahel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel "Sahel") regions of northern Central Africa, where some groups settled.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Eastern_Africa_2011,_pg._4-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-Eastern_Africa_2011,_pg._4) Farming began as part of the [Neolithic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_Revolution "Neolithic Revolution").[&#8202;](#ref-back-20-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-20) Initial farming of [white yam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dioscorea_rotundata "Dioscorea rotundata") progressed into [millet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millet "Millet") and [sorghum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorghum "Sorghum"), and before 3000BCE[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-21) the domestication of [African oil palm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elaeis_guineensis "Elaeis guineensis") improved the groups' nutrition and allowed expansion of the local populations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-22-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-22) This agricultural revolution, combined with a "Fish-stew Revolution", in which fishing began, and the use of boats, allowed the transportation of goods. Products were often moved in ceramic pots.[&#8202;](#ref-back-23-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-23)

The [Bouar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bouar "Bouar") Megaliths in the western region indicate an advanced level of habitation dating to the late [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic "Neolithic") (c.3500–2700 BCE).[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-24)[&#8202;](#ref-back-25-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-25) [Ironwork](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferrous_metallurgy "Ferrous metallurgy") developed in the region around 1000BCE.[&#8202;](#ref-back-26-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-26) The [Ubangian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubangian_languages "Ubangian languages") settled along the Ubangi River, while some [Bantu people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bantu_peoples "Bantu peoples") migrated from the southwest of Cameroon.[&#8202;](#ref-back-27-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-27)

[Bananas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banana "Banana") arrived in the region during the first millennium BCE[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-28) and added an important source of carbohydrates to the diet; they were also used in the production of alcoholic beverages. Production of copper, salt, dried fish, and textiles dominated the economic trade in the Central African region.[&#8202;](#ref-back-29-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-29)

### 16th–19th centuries

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=4 "Edit section: 16th–19th centuries")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/Sultan_and_his_wives_at_Bangassou%2C_1906.png/250px-Sultan_and_his_wives_at_Bangassou%2C_1906.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sultan_and_his_wives_at_Bangassou,_1906.png) The Sultan of [Bangassou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangassou "Bangassou") and his wives, 1906

In the 16th and 17th centuries, [slave traders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery "History of slavery") began to raid the region as part of the expansion of the [Saharan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahara "Sahara") and [Nile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile "Nile") slave routes. Their captives were enslaved and shipped to a wide range of places: the Mediterranean coast, Europe, Arabia, the Western Hemisphere, the slave ports and factories along the coasts of West and North Africa, and south along the Ubangui and [Congo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congo_River "Congo River") rivers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-30-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-30)[&#8202;](#ref-back-africanhistory.about.com-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-africanhistory.about.com) During the 18th century Bandia-Nzakara [Azande](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azande_people "Azande people") peoples established the [Bangassou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangassou "Bangassou") Kingdom along the Ubangi.[&#8202;](#ref-back-africanhistory.about.com-1)[\[29\]](#refnote-africanhistory.about.com) In the mid 19th century, the [Bobangi people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangi_language "Bangi language") became major slave traders and sold their captives to the Americas using the Ubangi to reach the coast.[&#8202;](#ref-back-32-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-32) In 1875, the [Sudanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudan "Sudan") sultan [Rabih az-Zubayr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabih_az-Zubayr "Rabih az-Zubayr") governed Upper-Oubangui, which included present-day Central African Republic.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Zubayr-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-Zubayr)

### French colonial period

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=5 "Edit section: French colonial period")\]

Main articles: [Ubangi-Shari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubangi-Shari "Ubangi-Shari"), [French Equatorial Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Equatorial_Africa "French Equatorial Africa"), and [Colonialism in the Central African Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonialism_in_the_Central_African_Republic "Colonialism in the Central African Republic")

The European invasion of Central African territory began in the late 19th century during the [Scramble for Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scramble_for_Africa "Scramble for Africa").[&#8202;](#ref-back-34-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-34) Europeans—primarily the French, [Germans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germans "Germans"), and [Belgians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgians "Belgians")—arrived in the area in 1885. France seized and colonized Ubangi-Shari territory in 1894. In 1911 at the [Treaty of Fez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Fes "Treaty of Fes"), France ceded a nearly 300,000km2 portion of the [Sangha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sangha_River "Sangha River") and [Lobaye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobaye_River "Lobaye River") basins to the [German Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire "German Empire") which ceded a smaller area (in present-day [Chad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chad "Chad")) to France. After [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I "World War I") France again annexed the territory. Modeled on [King Leopold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopold_II_of_Belgium "Leopold II of Belgium")'s [Congo Free State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congo_Free_State "Congo Free State"), concessions were doled out to private companies that endeavored to strip the region's assets as quickly and cheaply as possible before depositing a percentage of their profits into the French treasury. The concessionary companies [forced local people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corv%C3%A9e "Corvée") to harvest rubber, coffee, and other commodities without pay and held their families hostage until they met their quotas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fp1015-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-fp1015)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/De_Gaulle_Bangui_1940.jpg/250px-De_Gaulle_Bangui_1940.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:De_Gaulle_Bangui_1940.jpg) [Charles de Gaulle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle "Charles de Gaulle") in Bangui, 1940

In 1920, [French Equatorial Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Equatorial_Africa "French Equatorial Africa") was established, and Ubangi-Shari was administered from [Brazzaville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazzaville "Brazzaville").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Francophone_Africa_1997,_pg._111-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-Francophone_Africa_1997,_pg._111) During the 1920s and 1930s the French introduced a policy of mandatory cotton cultivation,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Francophone_Africa_1997,_pg._111-1)[\[34\]](#refnote-Francophone_Africa_1997,_pg._111) a network of roads were built, attempts were made to combat [sleeping sickness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_trypanosomiasis "African trypanosomiasis"), and [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant "Protestant") [missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mission_(Christianity) "Mission (Christianity)") were established to spread Christianity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-37) New forms of forced labour were introduced, and a large number of Ubangians were sent to work on the [Congo–Ocean Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congo%E2%80%93Ocean_Railway "Congo–Ocean Railway"). Through the period of construction until 1934 there was a continual heavy cost in human lives, with total deaths among all workers along the railway estimated in excess of 17,000 of the construction workers, from a combination of both industrial accidents and diseases including [malaria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaria "Malaria").[&#8202;](#ref-back-38-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-38) In 1928, a major insurrection, the [Kongo-Wara rebellion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kongo-Wara_rebellion "Kongo-Wara rebellion") or 'war of the hoe handle', broke out in western Ubangi-Shari and continued for several years. The extent of this insurrection, which was perhaps the largest anti-colonial rebellion in Africa during the interwar years, was carefully hidden from the French public because it provided evidence of strong opposition to French colonial rule and forced labour.[&#8202;](#ref-back-39-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-39) French colonization in Oubangui-Chari is considered to be the most brutal of the French Empire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-40-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-40)

In September 1940, during the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War "Second World War"), [pro-Gaullist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_de_Gaulle "Charles de Gaulle") French officers took control of Ubangi-Shari, and [General Leclerc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippe_Leclerc_de_Hauteclocque "Philippe Leclerc de Hauteclocque") established his headquarters for the [Free French Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Liberation_Army "French Liberation Army") in [Bangui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangui "Bangui").[&#8202;](#ref-back-41-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-41) In 1946 [Barthélemy Boganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barth%C3%A9lemy_Boganda "Barthélemy Boganda") was elected with 9,000 votes to the [French National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_National_Assembly "French National Assembly"), becoming the Central African Republic's first representative in the French government. Boganda maintained a political stance against racism and the colonial regime but gradually became disheartened with the French political system and returned to the CAR to establish the [Movement for the Social Evolution of Black Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movement_for_the_Social_Evolution_of_Black_Africa "Movement for the Social Evolution of Black Africa") ( _Mouvement pour l'évolution sociale de l'Afrique noire,_ MESAN) in 1950.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Zubayr-1)[\[31\]](#refnote-Zubayr)

### Since independence (1960–present)

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=6 "Edit section: Since independence (1960–present)")\]

In the [1957 Ubangi-Shari Territorial Assembly election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1957_Ubangi-Shari_Territorial_Assembly_election "1957 Ubangi-Shari Territorial Assembly election"), MESAN captured 347,000 of the 356,000 votes[&#8202;](#ref-back-42-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-42) and won every legislative seat,[&#8202;](#ref-back-43-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-43) which led to Boganda being elected president of the Grand Council of French Equatorial Africa and vice-president of the Ubangi-Shari Government Council.[&#8202;](#ref-back-44-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-44) Within a year, he declared the establishment of the Central African Republic and served as the first prime minister. MESAN continued to exist, but its role was limited.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kalck2005p136-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-Kalck2005p136) The Central African Republic was granted autonomy 1 December 1958, a status which meant it was still counted as part of the French Empire.[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-46)

After Boganda's death in a [plane crash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barth%C3%A9lemy_Boganda#Plane_crash "Barthélemy Boganda") on 29 March 1959, his cousin [David Dacko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Dacko "David Dacko") took control of MESAN. Dacko became the country's first president when the CAR formally received independence from France on 13 August 1960, a date celebrated by the country's [Independence Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_independence_days "List of national independence days") holiday.[&#8202;](#ref-back-47-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-47) Dacko threw out his political rivals, including [Abel Goumba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Goumba "Abel Goumba"), former prime minister and leader of [Mouvement d'évolution démocratique de l'Afrique centrale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Evolution_Movement_of_Central_Africa "Democratic Evolution Movement of Central Africa"), whom he forced into exile in France. With all opposition parties suppressed by November 1962, Dacko declared MESAN the official party of the state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-48-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-48)

#### Bokassa and the Central African Empire (1965–1979)

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=7 "Edit section: Bokassa and the Central African Empire (1965–1979)")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/Bokassa_with_Ceausescu_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-Bokassa_with_Ceausescu_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bokassa_with_Ceausescu_(cropped).jpg) [Jean-Bédel Bokassa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-B%C3%A9del_Bokassa "Jean-Bédel Bokassa"), self-crowned [Emperor of Central Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emperor_of_Central_Africa "Emperor of Central Africa")[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bokassa-1)[\[10\]](#refnote-Bokassa)

On 31 December 1965, Dacko was overthrown in the [Saint-Sylvestre coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Sylvestre_coup_d'%C3%A9tat "Saint-Sylvestre coup d'état") by Colonel [Jean-Bédel Bokassa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-B%C3%A9del_Bokassa "Jean-Bédel Bokassa"), who suspended the constitution and dissolved the National Assembly. Bokassa declared himself [President for Life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_for_life "President for life") in 1972 and named himself Emperor Bokassa I of the [Central African Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Empire "Central African Empire") on 4 December 1976. A year later, he [crowned himself in an expensive ceremony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronation_of_Bokassa_I_and_Catherine "Coronation of Bokassa I and Catherine").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Bokassa-2)[\[10\]](#refnote-Bokassa)

In April 1979, students protested against Bokassa's decree that all school pupils buy uniforms from a company owned by one of his wives. The government violently suppressed the protests, killing 100 children and teenagers. Bokassa may have been personally involved in some of the killings.[&#8202;](#ref-back-49-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-49) In September 1979, [France overthrew Bokassa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Caban "Operation Caban") and restored Dacko to power (subsequently restoring the official name of the country and the original government to the Central African Republic). Dacko was overthrown in a [1981 coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1981_Central_African_Republic_coup_d'%C3%A9tat "1981 Central African Republic coup d'état") by General [André Kolingba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Kolingba "André Kolingba").[&#8202;](#ref-back-50-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-50)

#### Central African Republic under Kolingba

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=8 "Edit section: Central African Republic under Kolingba")\]

Kolingba suspended the constitution and ruled with a [military junta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_junta "Military junta") until 1985. He introduced a new constitution in 1986, which was adopted by a nationwide referendum. Membership in his new party, the [Rassemblement Démocratique Centrafricain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Democratic_Rally "Central African Democratic Rally"), was voluntary. In 1987 and 1988, semi-free elections to parliament were held, but Kolingba's two major political opponents, [Abel Goumba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Goumba "Abel Goumba") and [Ange-Félix Patassé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ange-F%C3%A9lix_Patass%C3%A9 "Ange-Félix Patassé"), were not allowed to participate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-:0)

By 1990, inspired by the [fall of the Berlin Wall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Berlin_Wall "Fall of the Berlin Wall"), a pro-democracy movement arose. Pressure from the United States, France, and from a group of locally represented countries and agencies called GIBAFOR (France, the US, Germany, Japan, the EU, the [World Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Bank "World Bank"), and the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations "United Nations")) finally led Kolingba to agree, in principle, to hold free elections in 1992 with help from the UN Office of Electoral Affairs. After using the excuse of alleged irregularities to suspend the results of the elections as a pretext for holding on to power, Kolingba came under intense pressure from GIBAFOR to establish a "Conseil National Politique Provisoire de la République" (Provisional National Political Council) and to set up a "Mixed Electoral Commission", which included representatives from all political parties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-1)[\[49\]](#refnote-:0)

When a second round of elections was held in 1993, again with the help of the international community coordinated by GIBAFOR, Patassé won with 53% of the vote to Goumba's 45.6%. Patassé's party, the _Mouvement pour la Libération du Peuple Centrafricain_ (Movement for the Liberation of the Central African People), gained a [plurality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plurality_voting "Plurality voting") but not an [absolute majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority "Majority") of seats in parliament, which meant Patassé's party required coalition partners.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-2)[\[49\]](#refnote-:0)

#### Patassé government (1993–2003)

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=9 "Edit section: Patassé government (1993–2003)")\]

Patassé purged many of the Kolingba elements from the government, and Kolingba supporters accused Patassé's government of conducting a " [witch hunt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witch-hunt#Figurative_use_of_the_term "Witch-hunt")" against the Yakoma. A new constitution was approved but had little impact on the country's politics. In 1996–1997, reflecting steadily decreasing public confidence in the government's erratic behavior, three mutinies against Patassé's administration were accompanied by widespread destruction of property and heightened ethnic tension. In 1996 the [Peace Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Corps "Peace Corps") evacuated all its volunteers to neighboring Cameroon. To date, the Peace Corps has not returned to the Central African Republic. The 1997 [Bangui Agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangui_Agreements "Bangui Agreements") provided for the deployment of an inter-African military mission to the Central African Republic and re-entry of ex-mutineers into the government. The inter-African military mission was later replaced by a UN [peacekeeping force (MINURCA)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_1159 "United Nations Security Council Resolution 1159"). Since 1997, the country has hosted almost a dozen peacekeeping interventions, earning it the title of "world champion of peacekeeping".[&#8202;](#ref-back-fp1015-1)[\[33\]](#refnote-fp1015) In the 1998 parliamentary elections, Kolingba's party won 20 out of 109 seats. But the next year, despite widespread anger in urban centers over his corrupt rule, Patassé won a second term in the presidential election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-52)

In 2001 rebels stormed strategic buildings in Bangui in an [unsuccessful coup attempt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Central_African_Republic_coup_attempt "2001 Central African Republic coup attempt"). The army chief of staff, Abel Abrou, and General François N'Djadder Bedaya were killed, but Patassé regained the upper hand by bringing in at least 300 troops of the [Congolese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Republic_of_the_Congo "Democratic Republic of the Congo") rebel leader [Jean-Pierre Bemba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Pierre_Bemba "Jean-Pierre Bemba") and Libyan soldiers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-53-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-53) In the aftermath of the failed coup, militias loyal to Patassé sought revenge against rebels in many Bangui neighborhoods and incited unrest including the murder of many political opponents. Eventually, Patassé came to suspect that General [François Bozizé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois_Boziz%C3%A9 "François Bozizé") was involved in another coup attempt against him, which led Bozizé to flee with loyal troops to Chad. In March 2003, Bozizé launched a surprise attack against Patassé, who was out of the country. Libyan troops and some 1,000 soldiers of Bemba's Congolese rebel organization failed to stop the rebels and Bozizé's forces overthrew Patassé.[&#8202;](#ref-back-54-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-54)

#### Civil wars

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=10 "Edit section: Civil wars")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bb/Rebel_in_northern_CAR_01.jpg/250px-Rebel_in_northern_CAR_01.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rebel_in_northern_CAR_01.jpg) Rebel militia in the northern countryside, 2007

Bozizé suspended the constitution and named a new cabinet, which included most opposition parties. [Abel Goumba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Goumba "Abel Goumba") was named vice-president. Bozizé established a broad-based National Transition Council to draft a new constitution and announced that he would step down and run for office once the constitution was approved.[&#8202;](#ref-back-55-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-55)

In 2004, the [Central African Bush War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_Bush_War "Central African Bush War") began as forces opposed to Bozizé took up arms against his government. In May 2005, Bozizé won the presidential election, which excluded Patassé, and in 2006 fighting continued between the government and the rebels.[&#8202;](#ref-back-56-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-56) In November 2006, Bozizé's government requested French military support to help them repel rebels who had taken control of towns in the country's northern regions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc20061114-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-bbc20061114) Though the initial public details of the agreement pertained to logistics and intelligence, by December the French assistance included airstrikes by [Dassault Mirage 2000](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dassault_Mirage_2000 "Dassault Mirage 2000") fighters against rebel positions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-58-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-58)[&#8202;](#ref-back-bbc20061130-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-bbc20061130)

The Syrte Agreement in February and the Birao Peace Agreement in April 2007 called for a cessation of hostilities, the billeting of [FDPC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Front_of_the_Central_African_People "Democratic Front of the Central African People") fighters and their integration with FACA, the liberation of political prisoners, the integration of FDPC into government, an amnesty for the [UFDR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_of_Democratic_Forces_for_Unity "Union of Democratic Forces for Unity"), its recognition as a political party, and the integration of its fighters into the national army. Several groups continued to fight but other groups signed on to the agreement or similar agreements with the government. The only major group not to sign an agreement at the time was the [CPJP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convention_of_Patriots_for_Justice_and_Peace "Convention of Patriots for Justice and Peace"), which continued its activities and signed a peace agreement with the government in 2012.[&#8202;](#ref-back-60-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-60)

In 2011, Bozizé was reelected in an election widely considered fraudulent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CIA-1)[\[11\]](#refnote-CIA) In November 2012, [Séléka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9l%C3%A9ka "Séléka"), a coalition of rebel groups, took over towns in the CAR's northern and central regions. These groups reached a peace deal with Bozizé's government in January 2013, involving a power-sharing government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CIA-2)[\[11\]](#refnote-CIA) The deal later broke down, the rebels seized the capital in March 2013, and Bozizé fled the country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cnn20130324-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-cnn20130324)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nyt20130325-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-nyt20130325)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f1/Refugees_of_the_fighting_in_the_Central_African_Republic_observe_Rwandan_soldiers_being_dropped_off_at_Bangui_M%27Poko_International_Airport_in_the_Central_African_Republic_Jan._19%2C_2014_140119-F-RN211-760.jpg/250px-thumbnail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Refugees_of_the_fighting_in_the_Central_African_Republic_observe_Rwandan_soldiers_being_dropped_off_at_Bangui_M'Poko_International_Airport_in_the_Central_African_Republic_Jan._19,_2014_140119-F-RN211-760.jpg) [Refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refugee "Refugee") of the fighting in the Central African Republic, January 2014

[Michel Djotodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_Djotodia "Michel Djotodia") took over as president. Prime Minister [Nicolas Tiangaye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolas_Tiangaye "Nicolas Tiangaye") requested a UN peace, and on 31 May Bozizé was indicted for [crimes against humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crimes_against_humanity "Crimes against humanity") and incitement to genocide.[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-63) By the end of the year, there were international warnings of a "genocide"[&#8202;](#ref-back-64-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-64)[&#8202;](#ref-back-65-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-65) and fighting was largely reprisal attacks on civilians by Seleka's predominantly Muslim fighters and Christian militias called " [anti-balaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-balaka "Anti-balaka")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-GuardianNov13-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-GuardianNov13) By August 2013, there were reports of over 200,000 [internally displaced persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internally_displaced_persons "Internally displaced persons").[&#8202;](#ref-back-67-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-67)[&#8202;](#ref-back-68-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-68)

In February 2014, Secretary-General [Ban Ki-moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ban_Ki-moon "Ban Ki-moon") called on the [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council "United Nations Security Council") to deploy 3,000 troops to the CAR, bolstering the 6,000 African Union soldiers and 2,000 French troops already there, to combat civilians being murdered in large numbers. The Séléka government was said to be divided,[&#8202;](#ref-back-69-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-69) and in September 2013, Djotodia officially disbanded Séléka, but many rebels refused to disarm, becoming known as ex-Séléka, and veered further out of government control.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GuardianNov13-1)[\[64\]](#refnote-GuardianNov13) It is argued that the focus of the initial disarmament efforts exclusively on the Séléka inadvertently handed the anti-Balaka the upper hand, leading to the forced displacement of Muslim civilians by anti-Balaka in Bangui and western CAR.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fp1015-2)[\[33\]](#refnote-fp1015)

In January 2014, Michael Djotodia and Nicolas Tiengaye resigned as part of a deal negotiated at a regional summit in Chad.[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-70) [Catherine Samba-Panza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_Samba-Panza "Catherine Samba-Panza") was elected interim president by the National Transitional Council,[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-71) becoming the first female Central African president. In July 2014, following Congolese mediation efforts, Séléka and anti-balaka representatives signed a ceasefire agreement in [Brazzaville](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazzaville "Brazzaville").[&#8202;](#ref-back-72-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-72) By the end of 2014, the CAR was de facto partitioned, with the anti-Balaka in the southwest and ex-Séléka in the northeast.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fp1015-3)[\[33\]](#refnote-fp1015) In March 2015, [Samantha Power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samantha_Power "Samantha Power"), the U.S. ambassador to the United Nations, said 417 of the country's 436 mosques had been destroyed, and Muslim women were so scared to go out in public they were giving birth in their homes instead of going to the hospital.[&#8202;](#ref-back-73-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-73) On 14 December 2015, Séléka rebel leaders declared an independent [Republic of Logone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Logone "Republic of Logone").[&#8202;](#ref-back-74-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-74)

#### Touadéra government (2016–present)

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=11 "Edit section: Touadéra government (2016–present)")\]

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/War_in_Central_African_Republic.svg/250px-War_in_Central_African_Republic.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:War_in_Central_African_Republic.svg) Current military situation in Central African Republic

[Presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2015%E2%80%9316_Central_African_general_election "2015–16 Central African general election") were held in December 2015. As no candidate received more than 50% of the vote, a second round of elections was held on 14 February 2016 with runoffs on 31 March.[&#8202;](#ref-back-75-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-75)[&#8202;](#ref-back-76-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-76) In the second round, former Prime Minister [Faustin-Archange Touadéra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faustin-Archange_Touad%C3%A9ra "Faustin-Archange Touadéra") was declared the winner with 63% of the vote, defeating [Union for Central African Renewal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_for_Central_African_Renewal "Union for Central African Renewal") candidate [Anicet-Georges Dologuélé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anicet-Georges_Dologu%C3%A9l%C3%A9 "Anicet-Georges Dologuélé"), another former prime minister.[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-77) While the elections suffered from many potential voters being absent as they had taken refuge in other countries, the fears of widespread violence were ultimately unfounded, and the [African Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Union "African Union") regarded the elections as successful.[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-78) Touadéra was sworn in on 30 March 2016. No representatives of Séléka or the "anti-balaka" militias were included in the subsequently formed government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-79)

[Presidential elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020%E2%80%9321_Central_African_general_election "2020–21 Central African general election") were held on 27 December 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-80) Bozizé announced his candidacy but was rejected by the Constitutional Court, which held that he did not satisfy the "good morality" requirement for candidates because of an international warrant and United Nations sanctions against him for alleged assassinations, torture, and other crimes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-81) As large parts of the country were at the time controlled by armed groups, the election could not be conducted in many areas of the country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-82)[&#8202;](#ref-back-83-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-83) Some 800 of the polling stations, 14% of the total, were closed due to violence.[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-84) Three [Burundian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burundi "Burundi") peacekeepers were killed and two were wounded during the run-up to the election.[&#8202;](#ref-back-85-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-85)[&#8202;](#ref-back-UNNews_261220-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-UNNews_261220) Touadéra was reelected.[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-87)

Russian mercenaries from the [Wagner Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wagner_Group "Wagner Group") have supported Touadéra in the fight against rebels. Wagner Group has been accused of harassing and intimidating civilians.[&#8202;](#ref-back-88-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-88)[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-89) In December 2022, [Roger Cohen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Cohen "Roger Cohen") wrote in _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_York_Times "The New York Times")_ that "Wagner shock troops form a Praetorian Guard for Mr. Touadéra, who is also protected by Rwandan forces, in return for an untaxed license to exploit and export the Central African Republic's resources" and "one Western ambassador called the Central African Republic...a 'vassal state' of the Kremlin."[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-90)

Although the constitution initially limited presidency to two terms, Touadéra abolished constitutional term limits through a controversial referendum in 2023. He was reelected to a third term in December 2025.[&#8202;](#ref-back-91-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-91)[&#8202;](#ref-back-92-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-92) As of 2026, Russian military personnel are reportedly still heavily integrated into Touadéra's government, both from state-run [Africa Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Africa_Corps_(Russia) "Africa Corps (Russia)")[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-93) and from the independent remnants of the Wagner Group.[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-94)[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-95)

## Geography

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=12 "Edit section: Geography")\]

Main article: [Geography of the Central African Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_the_Central_African_Republic "Geography of the Central African Republic")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Republique_Centrafricaine_-_Boali_-_Chutes_de_Boali.jpg/250px-Republique_Centrafricaine_-_Boali_-_Chutes_de_Boali.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Republique_Centrafricaine_-_Boali_-_Chutes_de_Boali.jpg) Falls of Boali on the Mbali River[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/be/CARvillagefromthesky.jpg/250px-CARvillagefromthesky.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:CARvillagefromthesky.jpg) A village in the Central African Republic

The Central African Republic is a landlocked nation in the interior of the African continent. It is bordered by Cameroon, Chad, Sudan, South Sudan, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, and the Republic of the Congo. It lies between latitudes [2°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_parallel_north "2nd parallel north") and [11°N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/11th_parallel_north "11th parallel north"), and longitudes [14°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/14th_meridian_east "14th meridian east") and [28°E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/28th_meridian_east "28th meridian east").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-:1)

At 622,984 square kilometres (240,535sqmi), the CAR is the [world's 44th-largest country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area "List of countries and dependencies by area").[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-97) Much of the country consists of a flat or rolling plateau [savanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savanna "Savanna") approximately 500 metres (1,640ft) above sea level. In addition to the Fertit Hills in the northeast, there are scattered hills in the southwest. In the northwest is the Yade Massif, a granite plateau with an altitude of 348 metres (1,143ft). The Central African Republic contains six terrestrial ecoregions: [Northeastern Congolian lowland forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northeastern_Congolian_lowland_forests "Northeastern Congolian lowland forests"), [Northwestern Congolian lowland forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Congolian_lowland_forests "Northwestern Congolian lowland forests"), [Western Congolian swamp forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Congolian_swamp_forests "Western Congolian swamp forests"), [East Sudanian savanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Sudanian_savanna "East Sudanian savanna"), [Northern Congolian forest-savanna mosaic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Congolian_forest-savanna_mosaic "Northern Congolian forest-savanna mosaic"), and [Sahelian Acacia savanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahelian_Acacia_savanna "Sahelian Acacia savanna").[&#8202;](#ref-back-DinersteinOlson2017-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-DinersteinOlson2017)

Much of the southern border is formed by tributaries of the Congo River; the [Mbomou River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mbomou_River "Mbomou River") in the east merges with the [Uele River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uele_River "Uele River") to form the Ubangi River, which also comprises portions of the southern border. The Sangha River flows through some of the western regions of the country, while the eastern border lies along the edge of the Nile [watershed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drainage_basin "Drainage basin").[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[94\]](#refnote-:1)

Around 36% of the country is covered by forest, with the densest forest in the south. The forests are highly diverse and include commercially important species of [Ayous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triplochiton_scleroxylon "Triplochiton scleroxylon"), [Sapelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sapele "Sapele"), and [Sipo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entandrophragma "Entandrophragma").[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-99) The deforestation rate is about 0.4% per annum, and [lumber poaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_logging "Illegal logging") is commonplace.[&#8202;](#ref-back-100-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-100) The Central African Republic had a 2018 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index "Forest Landscape Integrity Index") mean score of 9.28/10, ranking it seventh globally out of 172 countries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FLII-Supplementary-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-FLII-Supplementary)

In 2008, Central African Republic was the world's least [light pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_pollution "Light pollution") affected country.[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-102)

The focal point of the [Bangui magnetic anomaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangui_magnetic_anomaly "Bangui magnetic anomaly"), one of the largest [magnetic anomalies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetic_anomaly "Magnetic anomaly") on Earth, is located in the capital.[&#8202;](#ref-back-LPI-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-LPI)

### Climate

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=13 "Edit section: Climate")\]

Main article: [Climate of the Central African Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_the_Central_African_Republic "Climate of the Central African Republic")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/78/Koppen-Geiger_Map_CAF_present.svg/330px-Koppen-Geiger_Map_CAF_present.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Koppen-Geiger_Map_CAF_present.svg) [Köppen climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification "Köppen climate classification") map of the Central African Republic

The climate is generally [tropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropics "Tropics"), with a [wet season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wet_season "Wet season") that lasts from June to September in the north, and from May to October in the south. During the wet season, rainstorms are an almost daily occurrence, and early morning fog is commonplace. Maximum annual precipitation is approximately 1,800 millimetres (71in) in the upper Ubangi region.[&#8202;](#ref-back-104-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-104)

The northern areas are hot and humid from February to May,[&#8202;](#ref-back-105-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-105) but can be subject to the hot, dry, and dusty [trade wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_wind "Trade wind") known as the [Harmattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harmattan "Harmattan"). The southern regions have a more equatorial climate, but they are subject to [desertification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertification "Desertification"), while the extreme northeast regions are [steppe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steppe "Steppe").[&#8202;](#ref-back-106-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-106)

### Biodiversity

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=14 "Edit section: Biodiversity")\]

Main article: [Wildlife of the Central African Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_the_Central_African_Republic "Wildlife of the Central African Republic")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/68/Dzanga.jpg/250px-Dzanga.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dzanga.jpg) [Dzanga-Sangha Reserve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzanga-Sangha_Reserve "Dzanga-Sangha Reserve")

In the southwest, the [Dzanga-Sangha National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dzanga-Sangha_Special_Reserve "Dzanga-Sangha Special Reserve") is located in a rainforest area. The country is noted for its population of [forest elephants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_forest_elephant "African forest elephant") and [western lowland gorillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_lowland_gorilla "Western lowland gorilla"). In the north, the [Manovo-Gounda St. Floris National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manovo-Gounda_St._Floris_National_Park "Manovo-Gounda St. Floris National Park") and [Bamingui-Bangoran National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bamingui-Bangoran_National_Park "Bamingui-Bangoran National Park") contain [leopards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_leopard "African leopard"), [lions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_African_lion "Central African lion"), [cheetahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudan_cheetah "Sudan cheetah") and [rhinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhinoceros "Rhinoceros"). The parks have been seriously affected by the activities of poachers, particularly those from Sudan, over the past two decades.[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-107)

[Forest cover](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_cover "Forest cover") is around 36% of the land area, equivalent to 22,303,000 hectares (ha) of forest in 2020, down from 23,203,000 hectares (ha) in 1990. In 2020, naturally regenerating forest covered 22,301,000 hectares (ha) and planted forest covered 2,000 hectares (ha). Of the naturally regenerating forest, 9% was reported to be [primary forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old-growth_forest "Old-growth forest") (consisting of native tree species with no clearly visible indications of human activity). In 2015, 91% of the forest area was reported to be under public ownership and 9% private ownership.[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-108)[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-109) In 2021, the rate of [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation "Deforestation") increased by 71%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-110)

## Government and politics

\[ [edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Central_African_Republic&action=edit&section=15 "Edit section: Government and politics")\]
